ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e126	Honourable co-representatives! Former PD Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland died after a long illness on July 25th this year. He was 74 years old. He represented Akershus Labour Party for 20 years at Stortinget, for a period ranging from 1973 to 1993. He was an alternate from 1969 to 1973. Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland was a social economist from the University of Oslo. He became economic director at Ahus, a position he had leave from all the years he was at Stortinget. He returned to the position in 1993. He has also worked in his professional career at the Norwegian Institute of Foreign Policy, in the municipality of Skedsmo and at the end of his career in the Landslag for public pensioners, LOP. Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland became early politically active. He was the study director of the Labour Party Student Team in 1965 and the president of the Skedsmo AUF in 1969. He was a member of the Norwegian Student Union Economics Committee. In the spring of 1966 he was a member of the Student Society's board. Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland had several prominent positions during his five periods on the Stortinget. He was a member of the Justice Committee in the first period. For two periods he sat on the Consumer and Administrative Committee, and he was a member of the Financial Committee for eight years. In the autumn of 1974 and in the autumn of 1980, Gulbrandsen Mykland was a delegate to the UN General Assembly. He was also a member of the Norwegian Parliamentary Delegation to the Nordic Council and member of the Norwegian Parliamentary Delegation to EFTA. In the Financial Committee, he was given the great responsibility of the rapporteur to unload the new tax reform that entered into force in 1992, through the Stortinget. Throughout this work, Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland led the peoples to broad agreement on the principles of a new tax system for businesses and individuals. Those who followed closely the work of the Finance Committee in this extensive and demanding work gave Gulbrandsen Mykland very good evidence of his work as rapporteur. He was, among other things, also the case-rapporteur for the Insurance Contracts Act of the Consumer and Administrative Committee. Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland was also very engaged in international work. After leaving the Stortinget in 1993, Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland was assigned to both the Refugee Council and the OSCE. He also served as an election observer in a number of countries and was active in AWEPA, the Parliamentary Network for Southern Africa. He was a real Nordic and was already in his youth secretary in the Nordic Association, Department Skedsmo. Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland participated in the political life of Skedsmo for a number of years, both before, during and after the time of the Storting. For many years he was a marked leader of the Skedsmo Workers' Party, and there at a time of great development and activity in Skedsmo and on the Stortinget. He was also head of the Akershu Labour Press. Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland was a proficient, libertarian and illegitimate politician. He never allowed his opinions to go over, even when these went across his own party's established policies. And he's probably one of the few who in Stortinget has taken a line on the council of state from his own party. Despite many demanding years in politics, Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland also found time for the family in leisure. We thank Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland for his efforts on the Stortinget and in Norwegian society in general, and we light peace on Thor-Eirik Gulbrandsen Mykland's memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e209	First, I would like to thank the Cooperation Committee on the matter we are going to deal with today, which concerns the repeal of one of two laws regulating pro-boking. The ban on pro-boxing was adopted in 1981 because of adverse medical effects, risk of acute and permanent brain damage and risk of death. In addition, it was suggested that professional boxing would take place in the present unhealthy economic interests. Professional boxing is prohibited by its own law, while other fighting activities that allow knockout are regulated by the knockout law. The Committee has carried out a consultation on the proposal, and in the hearing, the Medical Association, the Norwegian Neurosurgical Association and the Norwegian Sports Association were opposed to amending the law, while the Norwegian Professional Boxing Association and the Norwegian Boxing Association were in favour. The Committee is divided into several parts. A majority of five, including the chairman, support the proposal, while a minority of five are opposed to repealing the law. The Labour Party does not support the proposal. The main reason is the changes that the Council of State has already implemented in terms of security provisions. Amateur boxing operates in many clubs around Norway today, and we are positive about this, because we have had strict legislation, a code that, in our opinion, provided the necessary provisions for the safety and limitation of damage. Governments have recently amended this legislation, to major protests from medical environments and from sports organisations. A lifting of the Professional Boxing Act would in principle be to delegate to the Council of State the setting of the necessary security provisions and frameworks around professional boxing. Like heavy hearing bodies, we disagree with the changes that have already been implemented in the security regulations. The Labour Party therefore listens to an overall medical environment that is contrary to the bill. They have a knowledge-based basis to oppose, and there is an increased risk of harm. As the Medical Association says: ‘there is a difference in substance between the head injury caused in an activity which permits and honors knockout and an equivalent injury resulting from a purely sporting accident’. The government's own technical directorate, the Health Directorate, is also opposed to the changes. The same is true of Norwegian association for sports medicine and physical activity. The Labour Party listens to sport, which is both against the changes made by the Council of State and against lifting the ban on professional boxing. The Sports Association has justified this with several factors: firstly, with the head injuries, secondly, that the professional boxing associations do not have an anti-doping code approved by the international anti-doping body WADA, and thirdly with a lack of overview and action against fighting fixation. When the Workers' Party presented the Norwegian sports model, one of the principles that was established was that the sport should own its own events. It ensures that income is allocated to special associations and clubs so that young athletes can participate in the income generated. Professional boxing is organised by commercial actors. They're the ones who're left with the surplus. Youth in Norwegian book clubs will not benefit from the profits of professional struggles. That's what the commercial actors do. The proposal to allow professional boxing comes from a government that usually uses every opportunity to assert its commitment to knowledge. With all due respect, in this case, government parties have been harmed to put the entertainment value ahead of the knowledge value. Unfortunately, it seems that the proposal to repeal the law has a majority. Then it is Knockout to decide whether or not to authorise professional matches in Norway. A clear premise must be that the denominator demands that those with rights and responsibilities for the different titles, who are to organise matches in Norway, have formal cooperation with WADA and its legislation. Now that the Council of State is pricing as a member of WADA's board of directors, I expect that there will be the opening up of a joint proposal by the Christian People's Party, SV, the Center Party and the Workers' Party that will clarify exactly that. I will address the distributed proposal which reads as follows: ‘The Government is requesting that everyone who organises boxing matches in Norway has ratified the WADA Code and thus has a code approved by WADA.’

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e426	The government will thus facilitate professional boxing in Norway under the same legislation as other organised fighting activities that allow knockout. The organisation of professional boxing in Norway requires the repeal of the Professional Boxing Act. The Professional Code provides that participation in competition, demonstration or training in professional boxing is prohibited in Norway. The repeal of the Professional Boxing Act will result in professional boxing being regulated by one knock-out law and the corresponding knock-out regulation. The Government considers it appropriate to regulate all fighting activities, including professional boxing, by one and the same law. The government acknowledges that professional boxing, like other sports, poses a risk of injury. Nevertheless, the government considers that the risk of injury associated with professional boxing is not a sufficiently serious argument for maintaining a legal ban. The Government wants professional boxing to take place within the framework of a coherent national legislation that ensures the essential safety requirements of the activity. Safety requirements will not eliminate the risk of injury associated with the activity. Within the safety requirements set, it will be up to the individual practitioner to assess whether he or she wishes to be exposed to the risk of harm arising from the activity. The consequence of this is that health and safety issues become a private matter, where responsibility is placed on the individual exercise, to which Norway’s sports federations are very critical. They write in their replies to the hearing: ‘The liberalisation which the amendments to the rules of the Knockout Act entail for the sports organised under the NIF, including the Norwegian Boxing Association, challenges the value of Norwegian sport. It also represents a breach of the ethical leadership given for the top-notch sport in the last major sports event. Sport may not have a value basis that makes all health and safety matters a private matter and assigns responsibility to the individual performer.» They further write in their hearing response: ‘However, it is the State that sets out the external framework for what should be permitted as sport in our country. Under such a framework, the State at the same time sets limits on what should be prosecuted in criminal law. Injury resulting from death shall be exempt from criminal prosecution as long as it takes place within the framework of the law to which the approved sport is subject. This underscores the seriousness of the relaxation of the safety requirements of any of the combat events in question.’ They end with saying: ‘The responsibility lies with the legislator.’ That's us. The Committee has carried out a wide-ranging consultation in the light of the legislative proposals. In the hearing, the Norwegian Neurosurgical Association, the Norwegian Medical Association and the Norwegian Sports Association warned against the government's proposals. The proposals were supported by the Norwegian Boxing Association and the Norwegian Professional Boxing Association. When the ban was introduced in 1981, the main argument was the risk of medical adverse effects on practitioners. The followers of professional boxing will show that today medical surveillance is strict, including in the field of professional boxing. But it is not possible to stick under the chair that boxing can cause damage. It is impossible, in the view of the Christian People's Party, to ignore the many warnings of an overall medical profession about the short-term and long-term consequences of repeated blows to the head. There is a broad consensus on risks and adverse effects in the scientific field internationally, as well as in the Health Directorate and the Norwegian Medical Association. It is a cross-thinking that the government chooses not to listen to its own health policy directorate. The fight against health damage and the fight against doping has long been high on the sporting policy agenda in Norway and in a number of other countries. In this area, good cooperation has been developed between sport and the public, both nationally and internationally. The result is an increased demand for anti-doping work, and it is worth noting that today’s tone-indicating professional boxing unions are outside the organized sport controlled by WADA. This also applies to the Norwegian Professional Boxing Association. The health aspect and anti-doping work must weigh heavily, and the Christian People's Party therefore does not want to legalize a sport where the purpose is to harm the opponent, as is the case in professional boxing. We are therefore going to vote against the government's proposal, but of course for the proposal we ourselves have supported.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e700	Professional boxing has been banned in Norway since 1981. The reason for the imposition of the ban in 1981 was the risk of medical adverse effects on the performer, but a lot has happened since then. Europe has a more stringent licensing system than the rest of the world, and there have been no deaths in Europe — I am aware — in professional boxing in almost 30 years. Today, medical surveillance is strict, including in the field of professional boxing, which it should be. In the world context, Norway is in an exclusive company with only a limited number of countries, including Iran and North Korea, which prohibits professional boxing. Since 2002, the knockout law has been in force. While the Professional Boxing Act states that professional boxing is prohibited, the knockout law allows all other organised combat activities where knockout is permitted on application. In order to obtain such an authorisation, fighting activity must comply with specific safety provisions. Among other things, there is a requirement for the commander in charge of the fight, a fighter with the right to stop a fight, a health certificate, quarantine regulations and anti-doping work in accordance with the WADA code. Let me elaborate on this, because I have come to realize that there has been some doubt as to what this means. WADA Code does not require sports and sports organisations outside the Olympic movement to sign the WADA Code. This also applies to potential applicants for the organisation of professional knockout activities. However, the WADA Code allows sports and sports organisations outside the Olympic movement to apply for the WADA Code. But – and it’s important to stress – it’s up to WADA to decide, there’s nothing the Norwegian dump can do. What the government wants to ensure through this paragraph of the regulation is that those who are to be allowed to arrange knockout activities actually have a functioning anti-doping work. So the government has taken into account what is proposed in the distributed proposal, a proposal that I think should be voted on, because it is wrong. That's exactly what we've taken into account in this regulation, that it has to be arranged for you to have a functioning anti-doping work. With the proposed law repealing the Professional Boxing Ban Act, the government wishes to facilitate professional boxing in Norway. The Professional Boxing Act and the knockout Act cover mainly the same activities, have the same geographical scope and equal penalties. The repeal of the Professional Boxing Act means that professional boxing is regulated by the knock-out and related regulations. It will then be possible to apply for organised competitions and demonstration or tournament matches, even in professional boxing. The government considers it appropriate to regulate all fighting activities that allow knockout, including professional boxing, by one and the same law. Such coordination will be elucidating, simplifying and ensuring non-discrimination between the various combat activities that allow knockout, irrespective of the way in which the activity is identified. Repeal of the Professional Boxing Act does not imply a release release. An independent name still needs to be applied for approval. This approval number will determine, on the basis of an application, whether the applicant complies with all the requirements of the legislation. Professional boxing, like other knockout activities, leads to damage risk, and I think it's worth stressing: like other knockout activities. The government considers that the risk of injury associated with professional boxing is not a sufficiently serious argument for maintaining a specific legal ban. Nor does the prohibition have any effect on the ability of Norwegian practitioners to exercise sport abroad. The intention to protect the health of practitioners through the ban is thus limited today. The government no longer believes that there is a basis for a special law on professional boxing. The Knockout Act already provides for similar activities in the context of specific security provisions. Professional boxing should therefore be possible within the framework of a coherent national legislation which ensures the essential safety requirements for the activity. On this basis, the Government proposes to repeal the Professional Boxing Prohibition Act. Just a few comments on the present proposal. The WADA code, to which reference is made, is not a mutual agreement between two parties but an international code. It is the IOC Code, signed by sports organisations, including the Norwegian Sports Association. So you don't ratify WADA's legislation, you sign it. And it's up to WADA to decide who's going to have the opportunity to sign it.

It is not allowed to hit the back, it is not allowed to hit the back of the head, it is not allowed to hit the back of the head, and it is not allowed to hit the side of the body, i.e. hit the kidneys – it is only allowed to hit the front of an opponent. He went 53 professional matches. In one of the big fights he met one who was called the 'Argentinian bull'. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1162	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. 1st@item: inlistbox
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1176	The aim of this management audit report is to compare the stated objective of the Stortingen to full transition to electronic information exchange in the health sector with the actual results achieved. When Stortinget decided to introduce the Interaction Reform, it was very clear in the Inner East. 212 S for 2009-2010 from the Committee that the very success of the Interaction Reform would be largely due to the effective exchange of messages between the different entities, so that they could cooperate as closely as possible across the boundaries of the health and care sectors. We all agree that the sector can offer services of good quality, safe, without unnecessary time loss and at a high professional level. There is also full consensus that the use of already developed ICT systems is essential to achieve this. The need for an electronic message exchange has been repeated in all national ICT strategies since 1997. In this case, it appears that the Stortinget has been presented for the objectives set out in Interaction 2.0 — a national strategy that is the latest strategy for the 2008–2013 time span, the so-called national reporting promise, which was managed by the Health Directorate in the period 2008–2011, and the Notification Distribution programme, which was under the direction of Norsk Healthnet in the period 2012–2014. We must note that there is still a significant deviation from the objectives and that the exchange of information in healthcare companies is still largely on paper. An electronic part of the scope of the message does not take place on the basis of nationally accepted standards, with potentially impaired patient safety as a consequence. Similarly, there is no requirement for the content, structure and format of electronic messages in the sector. Although some units have come a long way, the practical use of these digital solutions is limited by the fact that the partners do not, and thus also the actors who have followed up loyally are forced to maintain communication on paper, and we come in the situation of double routines, i.e. both paper-based and digital routines in parallel. The government then announced in the 2011 State Budget in this forum – Stortinget – that all municipalities should have started electronic interaction with, among others. The specialist health service in the period 2008–2013, but what we can see, is that the actual target achievement at the end of the period, i.e. October 2013, was 34 pst. The Committee doubts whether, with such a low level of coverage as must have been the reality of the 2011 state budget being promoted, it could in some way have been realistic to achieve the objective presented in the Stortinget. We must also note that, when paper-based reporting systems are largely maintained, there are no key assumptions made by the Storting for effective patient treatment, nor have the objectives of the national strategy and national reporting obligations been achieved. As part of the work on this management audit, the Committee organised an audit hearing on 20 October this year in which the main actors and knowledge environments met. The Committee summarises the consultation there to the effect that it largely confirmed the Riksrevision study, i.e., more concisely and in concrete terms: Technical prerequisites for the closure of paper shipments have not been developed or put into use by all actors. There are no continuous standards for all forms of message exchange. Technical solutions developed have either not been put into use by all actors or are not used in line with national interaction architecture. The addressing solution for messages has until recently failed. The national testing and approval system operated by the Health Directorate has not been organised in such a way as to ensure that messages can be sent in a standardised format independent of the supplier. It is noted that the Ministry of Health and Welfare’s prior investigation, planning, management and follow-up have been unsatisfactory. The management and use of instruments by the Ministry of Health and Welfare has been too weak, taking into account the needs to achieve the objectives and in the light of identifiable en route problems. The Ministry has failed to make formal demands that the actors implement standardised solutions at the same time. The lack of formal requirements for the use of electronic message exchange and fragmented responsibilities has given actors the impression that the time of introduction has been optional, after which suboptimisation has taken place. The belief in behavioural changes through the influence of attitudes has been less effective. The Committee notes that the Ministry of Health and Welfare has not followed up the opinion of the Committee at the Innst. S No 291 for 2007-2008, cf. Document No 3:7 for 2007-2008, the National Audit Office’s study on ICT in hospitals and electronic interaction in healthcare, that the Ministry has to take an active responsibility for using the funding system to ensure progress and standardised solutions in the work to achieve electronic interaction between hospitals, municipalities and general practitioners. On this point, the Committee notes that the public sector in general has not developed sufficient incentives to reward innovation, whether it is using known technologies for new uses or new technologies in more traditional value chains, and that, in addition to this, it is usually not a lack of mature technology but of ordinary management and organisational competence that fails. The Committee notes that, in particular, the interest of the Directorate-General for Health in using the experience gathered by diffi from similar management projects has been low. The Committee notes that it is also in the field examined by the National Audit Office through the management audit report in question that the Health Directorate is primarily responsible for implementation and has not made an investment decision based on return claims – gain – and as a result is unlikely to have developed a profit implementation plan. The lack of momentum has also contributed to the defying of solutions when they finally arrive. Postponement means that the qualitative improvements in services resulting from the introduction of new ICT solutions are expected to harm users and patients in the healthcare sector. The Committee is concerned that the State is not adequately able to realise the societal benefits through the planned and coordinated deployment of ICT. It is the Committee's impression that many ICT projects in the public sector are characterised by time-consuming internal processes, orderly reports and well-intentioned intentions at the overall and principle level. Lack of decision-making, lack of ability to implement common and mandatory solutions, lack of understanding of organisational assumptions in the implementation of ICT are contributing reasons why opportunities available through technology are not exploited in the organisation. The Committee is of the opinion that narrow sectoral interests and traditional organisational sounding are at the root of large and important ICT investments. Lack of governance and overall management, insufficient opportunities for the exchange of information across and technology solutions that cater for the special interests of trade unions, not the needs of users, are part of this major problem picture. For the period covered by the report, 2008–2013, the Committee has highlighted the responsibility of the Ministry for the follow-up of the external agencies, in this case the Health Directorate, the regional health enterprises, etc. Similarly, the Committee would stress the active information obligation of the State Councils. With this in mind, the Committee considers that it should be reported back to the Stortinget with up-to-date information when the Council of State with the constitutional responsibility for the establishment sees that the objectives previously presented to the Stortinget cannot be achieved on the basis of the plan. The Committee considers it necessary to move from coordination through advice, guidance and recommended standards to central ICT governance. This must be done on the basis of a strong mandate, common architecture and committed cooperation. The Committee points out that there is a consistent experience that, in large parts of State activities, it is considered that self-development of technology solutions to be tailor-made for activities is best suited to efficient and user-friendly service production. However, the Committee would point out that experience goes in the opposite direction and that there is a need to use standard software as far as possible. It will also contribute to the more reasonable and continuous development of the solutions chosen, lower maintenance costs and less vulnerability. In this context, the Committee, acting unanimously, will make the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to adequately explain how the government will address the ICT challenges in the healthcare sector identified in this setting. Inner. 67 S [[2014-2015]] As I suggested, there is a unanimous committee that has gone to the step of giving an operational message along the way. We depart from the usual schedule to attach the management report to the protocol. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1299	Through the reporting by the National Audit Office, as has already been mentioned, we shall compare the stated objectives of the Grand Duchy of Luxembourg on the full transition to electronic information exchange in the health and care sector with the results achieved. When the Health and Welfare Committee in its time addressed the Interaction Reform and a new welfare reform, the importance of effective exchange of notifications between the different entities to cooperate in the health and care sectors was highlighted. It cannot be stressed enough that we expect the health and care sector to be able to provide quality services. The services shall be provided safely, without any unnecessary loss of time, and shall maintain a high professional level. A well-functioning ICT system is the key to achieving effective interaction between the various actors in the sector and will contribute to effective interaction and minimise time-loss. A wish to switch to electronic message exchange has been repeated in all national ICT strategies since 1997. The findings revealed by the National Audit Office's report are mildly disappointing. Since 1997, when an ICT strategy has requested a switch to electronic message exchange, it is not good enough for a communication exchange today, between healthcare companies, municipalities and medical agencies, to continue to be characterised by a high level of paper usage. Why doesn't this go any faster in the future? And why does one not get a uniform message exchange between all the actors in this sector? The National Audit Office has pointed out that there are clear weaknesses in the planning, management and follow-up of the work on electronic message exchange by the Ministry of Health and Welfare. The National Audit Office also points out that the Ministry of Health and Welfare’s use of instruments has not been adapted to the objectives, status and challenges in this area. The background to the Riksrevisions report is based on collected data for the 2008–2012 period. In order to obtain an update of the state and additional comments from the sector and industry, the Committee, as mentioned above, had a wide-ranging consultation with many actors in this sector as well as external actors, which has given the Committee a good indication of what the status is right now and what issues we are facing. The hearing confirmed in all respects the findings of the Riksrevision investigation. There's a whole bunch of points one could draw out here, but I'll limit myself to including some of the notes that I think are important to notice: There are no continuous standards for all forms of message exchange. Technical solutions developed have either not been put into use by all actors or are not used in line with national interaction architecture. The national testing and approval system, operated by the Health Directorate, has not been organised in such a way as to ensure that messages can be sent in a standardised, supplier-independent format. The Ministry of Health's planning, management and follow-up have been unsatisfactory. The Ministry has failed to make formal demands that the actors implement standardised solutions at the same time. The different health establishments appear to have considered the time of introduction optional and this has led to suboptimisation. The undersigned was well ten years ago the director of a private hospital in the West, and I recognise the challenges of electronic interaction at the time, both through the National Audit Office's report and through the consultation that the Committee has had. Something has happened in this decade, the situation is not black and white at all, but the impression of the main issues is, in my opinion, the same today as it was then. I find it depressing that in 2014 we have not come so far that we have an ICT solution that provides good and effective flow of messages across and between all areas of the health and care sector, for the benefit of users of these services, and as expected by the Storting through many repeated policy decisions. In the future, one must now move to central ICT governance. There must be a strong mandate, common architecture and commitment. Effective interaction through electronic message exchange is an overly important objective for individual undertakings and professional interests to run their own races. I expect the Minister of Health and Care to take overall control of the ICT sector in the health and care sector and to ensure that the Health Directorate becomes a central provider of pre-mistakes and to push for an electronic, effective notification transmission in the health and care sector, as we expect, to become a reality within a reasonable period of time. I assume that the council itself will comment on this. Otherwise, I refer you to the rapporteur's detailed statement. The Contracting Party shall fully align itself with the unanimous recommendation of the Committee in this matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1344	From the launch of the first national ICT strategy in the health sector in 1997, a stronger national governance and coordination in the field of ICT has been requested by the EEA. So the proposal that a unanimous committee today is behind is actually made at regular intervals – with questions related to – over a period of almost 15 years. It's no wonder that someone today expresses impatience that we're still where we are. In all strategies since then, electronic message exchange has been a priority solution for an electronic interaction for the efficient and reliable exchange of patient information. The National Audit Office’s study, which we are debating today, has been aimed at assessing the status of the introduction of electronic message exchange and the simultaneous status of the liquidation of parallel paper procedures. Furthermore, the level of governance of national authorities and the organisation and follow-up of the objectives of efficient electronic interaction are examined. The study indicates that a great deal of competence has been developed in the health sector in this area, but the most striking thing is that the degree of implementation varies greatly, both among health businesses, in municipalities and in medical offices. Missing standards mean that the exchange of electronic messages takes place using locally adapted solutions. The requested stronger national governance is still very little in place. Common content and structure requirements are still not laid down. Double procedures are maintained: even where one has used electronic messages, patient information is still widely sent on paper. In other words, there is a lack of trust in the system that you are working on and that you have wanted to develop. Of the many findings presented by the National Audit Office, it is particularly emphasised that one in advance of the 2008–2013 strategy period had not systematically clarified the technical status of the various actors in the health and care sector. It was too easy to obtain up-to-date knowledge of the technical and organisational assumptions of the actors. In other words, there was a lack of a sufficient and sufficient basis for decision-making in order to follow up the objectives. It is also worth noting that, according to the Riksrevision, one has also not had enough focus on profit realization. A visualisation of the benefits would be important strategic approaches that could just create increased focus and motivation on the overall purpose of the introduction and use of ICT. It is also worth noting that a fragmented division of responsibilities has made the implementation of solutions difficult in line with national objectives. There has been too much up to the different actors how to solve the challenges. The national governance has been lacking. This has also led to the fact that the use of the instrument has not become as powerful as could be expected from the aim of Stortinget that all health companies, municipalities and medical offices should have established electronic notification exchanges by the end of 2014. So, although a clear record of a positive increase in the use of electronic messages has been made, the Council of State also underlines – we note – the need for the development and introduction of national notification standards between the different actors, as well as the fact that there has been too fragmented responsibility for the organisation of ICT in the health and care sectors. In addition to the fact that national targets in this area have not been achieved, perhaps some of the most serious in the situation is that this could result in a potentially impaired patient safety and less effective patient treatment. Double routines are rarely a type of quality assurance that contributes to something other than multiple time thieves and possible misunderstandings. There is a picture that the hunt for the best solutions has been too much left to individual actors. Instead of learning from the experiences of others — both nationally and not least outside the country’s borders — solutions have been chosen that have both been cost-effective and have led to a significant downturn in time. There is therefore reason for concern that another state-owned ICT project will not be realised according to the plan and national objectives. A strong mandate, central governance, focus on profit-reduction and right of access – not least the right to have tools, which can provide sufficient commitment in cooperation between actors – seems to be absolutely necessary and crucial to address the ICT challenges in healthcare. We, in the Christian People's Party, too, fully support the proposal of a unanimous committee, and I refer to the thorough examination of the rapporteur.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1386	Unfortunately, it is not new that large public ICT investments crack apart in terms of both time and money. The defense, the police, new emergency network, Nav, the state road system – there is no lack of examples. Electronic message exchange is hardly going to be the last example. ‘The police are slaughtering their own dataset’ – that was the title in the Official Journal of Bergen on 8 August 2013. The incidence continued with: ‘The police have spent billions on computer systems, but do not know what the money is spent on. It establishes a new police analysis.’ The red-green government set up a selection in November 2012 to analyse the challenges of the Norwegian police. In the final report presented at the end of June 2013, the Directorate of Police was criticised for the lack of management of investment in the ICT sector. The analysis reveals that the billions have cleared up without any control over what you have spent the money on. In total, 4.3 billion has been used. ICT spending in the last five years – i.e. until 2013. Of this, 1.3 billion. Roar Fosse, who in 2013 was the head of the Police Joint Union in Hordaland, said: ‘One of the main problems is that the systems do not talk to each other. We must therefore use time to record the same information both three and four times." If we go out of the country a little bit, these aren't unknown challenges either. Already in 2001, Denmark had a similar number of experiences with state-owned ICT projects in line with what we have had in Norway in recent years. The so-called Bonnerup Committee in Denmark presented in 2001 the report «Experts from state-owned IT projects – how do you do it better?». In the report it says, something prophetic: «In other words, the time of major public IT projects has only just begun. This means that we have seen only the top of the problems that the projects are at risk of encountering.' I guess that's where we're in Norway. We face many challenges related to ICT projects. One of the problems is interaction between actors who are on completely different digital planets and platforms, such as municipalities and hospitals, or permanent doctors and hospitals. In the health sector, part of the challenge, or problem, is also a large number of actors or stakeholders. Unfortunately, the patient’s interest is not so easy to see or hear in this image. On the other hand, there are a very large number of state stakeholders. Just listen to what state actors are involved in the so-called E-Health Group to contribute to more informed decisions, to a large pool of efforts and to a clear anchoring of responsibilities and implementation in the field of ICT actions in the health and care sector: it is the Ministry of Health and Health at the Council of Ministers, it is the top leaders of the Ministry of Municipal and Modernisation, the Directorate of Health, the National Institute of Public Health, the Directorate of Administration and ICT, the four regional health companies, the Norwegian Healthnet SF, the National ICT and the National Centre for Interaction and Telemedicine, and it is KS, representing 428 municipalities. In addition, we have now received Health Vest RHF, which has been given overall national responsibility for ICT in the hospital sector. So it's an innumerable number of actors and stakeholders. Many of the challenges and problems of public ICT projects stem from the conditions under which the projects will operate, and to a small extent from the technology itself. Promises, rules, organisation, funding and fragmentation are some of the challenges we're talking about. The representative Lundteg took up management and organisation, and there are several people who have talked about it. We politicians probably have to take part in the responsibility for the challenges and problems. We're, to the most extent, part of the leadership group here. Another part of the challenge picture is the procurement law and its practice. We have a rather rigid Norwegian interpretation of EU tendering rules, and it is also one of the handicaps to the effectiveness of public ICT projects. A challenge related to this is also the annual government budget and allocation systems that we have, which make it difficult to implement long-term and cross-cutting ICT projects. Here we need to challenge ourselves to become better at using procurement laws and different forms of competition, such as competitive dialogue, design contests and double tenders, where design and innovation are more focused at an early stage. A third significant challenge in what we are talking about here — the health sector — is standardisation. Of course, technical standards for ICT equipment and digital infrastructure are part of this, but in the health sector, medical terminology is very central. Australia was one of the countries that founded medical terminology and secured it. They have registered more than 300,000 tradesmen in the health sector. It is clear that when we are going to enter a technical paradigm shift from paper-based to digital communication, it is essential and essential that medical terminology and practice are recorded, clarified and unambiguous for digital use as far as possible. Here we still have a long way to go in Norway to structure medical terminology. Not least we see it in electronic patient records, where there is a significant job left to structure that terminology. Denmark is another example — I mentioned Australia and also in Denmark. There are two of the most advanced countries in the field of health digitisation and digitalisation in general in the public sector, from which there is something to learn. After 2001, Denmark has continued to provide reports in the public sector on ICT projects. It's pretty interesting to read that they'll try to be more offensive about taking more risk, focusing more on change, and doing a little bit more paradigm shifts. I see that in Norway too we have had political ambitions about it, but that we have not rigged organisations, directorates, departments and other actors for it. It is a debate I would like to welcome: that we, both in this Chamber and around local governments and other public political bodies, take up the debate on how offensive we can be in order to provide users with better tools, and also to provide ourselves with more efficiency. But what they also say in Denmark is that they have too much political focus on control, and that it creates a too large and detailed pattern of requirement specifications, which also seem to be inhibiting and not developing. It also points to the need for more central decision-making skills, and it is probably also important in Norway that we seek a greater degree of decision-making competence and obtain a more comprehensive ICT architecture for Norway, where a digital health architecture is particularly important. Management and education are also part of this, and as politicians, we also need to discuss what leadership we should be involved in, and what laws, rules and funding systems we should facilitate, in order to achieve the ambitions we have. I see that the committee has spent a lot of time discussing incentives, but little time discussing penalties. Why municipalities have not introduced this is one of the discussions that I think the committee has gone too little into. The cooperation reform has elements of penalties in itself, including: when ready-to-print patients stay in hospital, municipalities have to pay NOK 4000. Similar sanctions may also be considered in the ICT sector. Back to Australia: The Health and Care Committee was visiting there in the fall, and one of the success factors I want to say that we experienced in Australia, was that they have a very tight management and decision-making competence for their commitment to e-health. They use a government directorate to drive out both the technologies and the skills tools for those who are going to use the technology. There is also little discussion in the Committee’s position — how to educate everyone in the health sector to implement these tools. They used a lot of simulators to train their health professionals, and among other things. decision-makers were not allowed to use new technology until they had been through a simulator. They did not then have access to the financial instruments. In Australia, too, NEHTA, which is the State Directorate, has ownership of all critical national electronic health infrastructure. They use it to a large extent in private to contribute, but it is NEHTA – this Australian directorate – who actually programs and sets the standards themselves, while dropping in the private ones by tender to do development jobs separately. In Norway, we have done it almost the other way around: we have called for tenders to private actors, both development jobs and, most importantly, policy developments, and we are running after them to coordinate. And if the private actors have programmed themselves into monopolies, we're facing a big challenge. In particular, in the area of community health services, we experience some of these challenges, but in part we also do so in the hospital sector, where we now have two actors within the four different health companies as central system operators. We are faced with a good deal of challenges associated with this, and taking on the national leadership trough is, to a large extent, a political responsibility. I do not feel that we are currently aiming to take the discussions in Norway and find the actor image that leads to implementation. And to mention that, Australia is fully at Norway's height when it comes to using electronic patient records, but they haven't chosen to phase all patients into it. They believe that the safety risk for it is too high, and will use the next three years to develop the system with a limited number of patients inside it. Here we have good reason to follow up on other countries that have the same challenges, which have come about about as far as we are, but choose to do so in other ways. We also have good examples in Norway. The tax haven and the Altinn are an example of a significant success in Norwegian ICT management. Why, across the board, we can't learn from good examples, is also a little mystery. And when we have a directorate for ICT and acquisitions, we should be more able to use it to solve these major challenges.

I believe that the Riksrevision has thoroughly addressed the issues related to electronic message exchange. Both the report and the hearing of the Control and Constitutional Committee pointed to important structural, legal and economic issues that need to be addressed. I will repeat one of my messages from the hearing in the committee. The National Audit Office’s investigation was finalised in 2012. At that time, the project in question was re-planned, and in the period after 2012 there has been a good development in the sector. We now see that most of the targets are achieved, but later than initially assumed. All municipalities, except five, are now using electronic message exchange. More than 600 000 electronic messages are exchanged each day. The current situation is significantly better than when health professionals used only paper and mail. It says health professionals themselves, and the Health Inspectorate also places emphasis on this in its latest annual report. Electronic services and solutions bring great benefits to actors. However, we still have a number of challenges – challenges that are demanding individually, and which because of their coherence also reinforce each other. I want to make it clear that the government's goal is to create the patient's health service. A prerequisite for this is an offensive approach to these issues. We have to go from word to action. In order to know what steps to take, we also need to know where we stand and what the history is. The experience presented by the National Audit Office and the sector is therefore very useful in strengthening the ICT area. It's granted an increase of 80 million. kr to ICT in the State budget for 2015. One of the things I took up early as a health minister was ordering three Health Directorate reports to describe the current ICT situation in the health sector. The reports would describe an overall challenge picture, an analysis of ICT work in the specialist healthcare service and an analysis of the Norwegian supplier market for electronic patient records. I have received the analyses, and they confirm much of the content of the National Audit Office's report. Reference is made to the need for a significant strengthening of the efficiency of implementation in order to ensure more coherent and predictable ICT development. The sector depends on a much better common resource use. This requires stronger governance and a stronger use of instruments. The Government has taken action in these areas. A year ago, I announced a full review of today’s organisation of ICT in the healthcare sector. I believe it is necessary to make changes in the organisation and allocation of responsibilities in order to ensure effective management and resource use. I therefore want to strengthen national governance. I also see this as an important point for the Committee. ICT measures have been set up under the auspices of the Health Directorate, which is becoming important in order to achieve the objectives of the patient’s healthcare. I believe the time has come to assess whether the national governance of key ICT actions should be further strengthened. I have therefore entrusted the Health Directorate with the task of assessing the sustainable organisation of the ICT area, which is rigged for new roles and responsibilities. One of the models to be assessed by the Directorate-General for Health is the establishment of a separate management body for Health-ICT. Regardless of the model recommended, the aim is to establish a robust organisation capable of managing national development and responsibility for ICT. In addition, there is a need for an organisation that can develop and manage relevant governance instruments such as legislation and financial instruments. The Health Directorate will provide a report on this in 2015. However, organisational changes are not sufficient alone. As the Riksrevision points out, the use of the instrument also needs to be strengthened. The Health Directorate has therefore also been entrusted with the task of establishing cooperation with municipalities, regional health companies and Norsk Healthnet SF, where it is intended to assess new state instruments in order to enhance the implementation capacity of the ICT field. Work shall include assessments relating to: new models for financing ICT across actors in the sector setting up ICT development joint decision-making processes covering the whole sector’s needs for new or modified legal and regulatory homes in order to implement state leadership for ICT development and regulate actors’ obligations to follow them up; The National Audit Office and the Committee have further pointed out that there is a lack of a national interaction architecture, that technical solutions have not been implemented, and that there has been a lack of standards and requirements. Developments have been overly voluntary. It has therefore been important for the government to facilitate the provision of better legal framework conditions, both for technological development and through clearer requirements. I am therefore pleased that this year the Stortinge has adopted the necessary changes to the central legislation relating to the field of ICT. The old Health Register Act was divided into two laws in June – a new health record for secondary use of health data and a new patient record law for primary use, i.e. health care. These laws shall enter into force on 1 January 2015. The laws ensure a more appropriate regulation of the processing of health data and ensure higher data protection and information security requirements. The legislation facilitates the development of modern solutions so that the necessary health information can be exchanged better, faster and safer between health professionals in need. At the same time, patients are monitored for their own information. A common identity and access management solution shall be established to facilitate interaction and information sharing between businesses. It is in the next year's budget allocated 55 million. kr for this work. Together with the new Patient Records Act, I will adopt a regulation on access to health information between businesses. The Regulation regulates one way of sharing information between establishments and sets requirements that must be met in order to allow health professionals in one establishment to read the relevant journal information into the systems of another establishment. A code for ICT standards is now being worked on. We're going away from volunteering. The Regulation is to be incorporated into the new Patient Records Act and will clarify to suppliers and healthcare the ICT systems to be used. This Regulation shall be adopted in February 2015. Experience has shown us that today’s architecture – with electronic messages sent from the actor to the actor – is complicated. The feedback from the sector is that we cannot achieve the ambitious objectives of one citizen — one journal, as set out in the Storing Communication, with today’s architecture. It is important to note that the exchange of notifications has been the only legitimate solution for communication of health information in general until the new legislation enters into force. The Health Directorate and the sector’s actors are investigating the future health information system. In this work, it is important to include both national and international experience and solutions. Health professionals need to get the best tools we can offer. The Health Directorate will deliver a preliminary study report in autumn 2015. It's set at 20 million. NOK for this work until next year. The National Audit Office’s report has highlighted key issues that need to be addressed if we are to achieve the ambitious goals that the Stortinget has agreed to in the field – issues where the government is well in the process of implementing measures. However, I would like to reiterate that the objectives of the Riksrevision project have been evaluated, for all practical purposes have been achieved. Today’s electronic message exchange solutions give, as mentioned at the outset, benefits – both for patients and health professionals. But it's more to pick up, both in the short and long term. It is therefore important to have two thoughts in your head at the same time. In the short term, in my governance dialogue, I will ensure that further improvements and benefits of electronic message exchange are put in place. At the same time, we now have to work hard and follow up on the long-term measures and consider further action as a result of the investigation of one citizen – one journal. Of course, I will follow up the clear decision that the majority of the committee invites the Stortingen to do today, and come back with a report to the Stortingen on the status and measures. It is important for me, after hearing the debate, to stress that the ambitions of Interaction 2.0 2008–2013 are based on electronic message exchange. These are the ambitions that the National Audit Office's report has evaluated, which is the case today. Since then, the Stortinget has joined even more ambitions for the field through the processing of the Storing Message. It is not possible to achieve the ambitions contained in the message One incorporates — one journal, based on the technological developments on which Interaction 2.0 is based, related to electronic message exchange. Then we have to move on to a new generation of technological solutions. Such solutions were not lawful under the previous legislation. Therefore, it was essential to put in place the new legal basis for the work to which the Stortinget joined in June. It is a prerequisite for achieving the objectives set out in the Single European Act — one journal, to which Stortinget has also joined.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1595	The case's rapporteur is Kenneth Svendsen, but the file rapporteur will be kept by Helge Thorheim.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1598	This case concerns the National Audit Office’s study on child poverty, and the survey covers the period 2002–2013. The aim of the study has been to assess the extent to which state instruments and measures implemented in municipalities contribute to reducing the impact of poverty on children and young people. The background to the survey is that the Stortinget has repeatedly expressed the need to strengthen the response to child poverty. The size of the population has shown that child poverty in Norway is largely about children experiencing social exclusion due to the economic situation of the family. According to the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child, children are entitled to participate in games and recreational activities. From 2002 onwards, there have been several action plans and action plans against child poverty. During the same period, the proportion of children in low-income families has increased. It has reinforced the need for instruments aimed at economically disadvantaged children and young people. The main findings of the National Audit Office are: Several children grow up in low-income families. Many municipalities do not do enough to allow poor children to participate socially. State efforts to reduce the impact of poverty among children and young people are not well coordinated. The needs of children are not well enough identified when parents apply for social assistance. The National Audit Office recommends that the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion contribute to strengthening the efforts of municipalities to establish more and better measures to provide poor children and young people with good conditions for social participation. For example, the Ministry should: assess how municipalities can use the competence of the IMDi for children and young people with a migrant background in their work to ensure that it is possible for all children and young people to participate in society. The National Audit Office also recommends that the Ministry assess whether more knowledge about good solutions and methods can help municipalities reach more poor children and young people. The National Audit Office further recommends that the Ministry contribute to better coordination of efforts to reduce the consequences of poverty among children and young people. For example, the Ministry should: assess whether, together with the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs, it can: contribute to greater cooperation between directorates whose tasks and instruments are targeted at vulnerable children and young people, coordinating and facilitating the use of State grant schemes, clarifying the cases in which the Child Protection Act is to be applied, and when using the Law on Social Services in Nav, when children need financial support for leisure activities, making the use of State instruments more visible to children and young people better opportunities for social participation; The Committee points out that the proportion of children from low-income families has increased from 5 pst. to 8 pst. in the last 10 years. In 2012, this was 78 000 children, and half of them again have immigrant backgrounds. There are several reasons why the proportion of poor children has increased. Trends in income have led to an increase in the low income threshold over the last few years. Low income families are defined as families with an average net income of less than 60 pst over a period of three years. of the median income, i.e. the middle income. Other factors include increased immigration, more single dependants and a lack of regulation of public benefits. However, lack of professional participation is considered to be the main cause of child poverty. Therefore, the most effective means of reducing child poverty is to ensure that more people get into work. The Committee has noted that the government has launched a review of Nav, one of the aims of which is to get more of those who receive support from Nav to work. The Committee points out that many municipalities find it difficult to know who needs action and how the measures should be designed to reach the target audience. Only 10 pst. of the municipalities have evaluated the measures they have taken against child poverty, and one in four municipalities has not surveyed the living conditions of the inhabitants, as the social services law provides. In 2011, almost 60,000 children lived in households receiving social assistance. It is then reasonable to assume that there is a high correlation between those who receive social benefits and those who are defined as poor. The Committee agrees with the Riksrevision that the Nav Offices must be expected to have the necessary prerequisites to meet the requirements for monitoring and individual assessments of the children concerned, in order to expect a clear improvement in the situation in the coming years. The National Audit Service recommends that the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion contribute to strengthening the efforts of municipalities to establish more and better measures to ensure that poor children and young people can participate in social activities together with other children. The Committee agrees. The Committee has also noted that the Riksrevision considers that there is a need for better coordination of State grants against child poverty. Today, the same measures in municipalities are financed by different State grant schemes. In addition, administrative work related to the schemes needs to be simplified at both municipal, county and state level. The Committee is pleased that, in its reply to the Riksrevision, the Council of State states that it will follow up the recommendations to coordinate and simplify the use of State grant schemes. Following the National Audit Office’s assessment, the IMDi has competence that municipalities can benefit from in the field of measures that may include poor children and young people with a migrant background in social activities, and asks the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion to assess how this competence can be involved in countering the impact of child poverty on immigrants. The Committee is pleased that the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion and the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs will jointly develop common guidelines for cooperation and sharing of responsibilities between the Ministry of Child Protection and the Administration of Labour and Welfare, Nav. The Committee asks the Riksrevision to report back on whether the proposed measures and instruments have wished to be effective. With this, I would recommend the unanimous recommendation of the Committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1687	In recent years, poverty has come back on the agenda in a stronger way. Several have become aware that poverty affects not only people in other countries but also more people and more children in Norway. The aim of the National Audit Office’s study has been to assess the extent to which State instruments and measures implemented in municipalities have contributed to reducing the impact of poverty on children and young people. The National Audit Office’s survey and conclusions are in many ways depressing, for the survey shows that from 2002 to 2012, the proportion of children in low-income families increased from 5 to 8 pst. In other words, this implies that in 2012 there were 78 000 children living in households with an average income of less than 60 pst. of the median income, half of which has a migrant background. Over these years, different governments have provided action plans and allocated money to actions of different kinds. Much is being done in municipalities around the country. But despite the measures, despite the focus on poverty, the proportion and number of poor children have increased over the period. This cannot be described as anything but discouraging. At the same time, we do not know what the outcome would have been without the action plans and measures. However, the results show that the measures do not appear to have had the desired and expected good enough effect. The national audit points to several reasons for the increase in the number of poor children. The evolution of income has led to a higher low income threshold. Increased immigration, several single low-income dependants, many public transfers, among others child benefits have not been regulated in accordance with wage and price developments and lack of professional participation. The latter is probably the most important cause of poverty. Action to increase the number of people in work is therefore essential in order to achieve a sustained improvement in the income situation of those in the poverty group. Other studies we have examined in the Control and Constitutional Committee have shown that efforts in this area have also not been good enough. At the same time, we know that not everyone is able to work healthily. It is therefore important that social security and other financial support schemes also have a size to live off and allow children to participate in activities in the same way as other children. The National Audit Office’s survey shows that many municipalities do not do enough to allow poor children to participate in social life, and that the needs of the children are not adequately addressed when parents apply for social assistance. Only one in five municipalities had e.g. reduced parental benefit schemes in kindergartens and free tickets for swimming pools. Many municipalities find it difficult to know which children need action and what action should be taken to reach those who need it. Only a few of the municipalities have surveyed the living conditions of the inhabitants, as the social law suggests. And even fewer have evaluated the measures they have in their municipality. This probably means that many municipalities do not have sufficient basic information to assess what measures they should take to improve the situation of children in poor families. Local knowledge is essential in order to take the good and correct actions. The National Audit Office also points out that the state effort is not well coordinated. In particular, this appears to be the case at the directorate level, between Nav, the Directorate for Children, Youth and Family Affairs and the Directorate for Integration and Diversity. It is strange that according to the study, the IMDI, the Integration and Diversity Directorate, is little involved in the work against child poverty, when at the same time one knows that about half of the children in low-income families have a migrant background. I would like to believe that the IMFI has just a competence both in the state and in the municipalities could have made better use of in this regard, and I expect this from the Council of State. The National Audit Office recommends that the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion contribute to strengthening the efforts of municipalities to establish more and better measures to provide poor children and young people with good conditions and social participation. They also recommend that efforts to reduce the impact of poverty among children and young people be better coordinated centrally. As I perceive the Council's reply to the Riksrevision, I assume that she will just follow up these recommendations. For the Christian People's Party, combating poverty has been a field of activity in our alternative budgets and through other proposals in the United Kingdom over many years. It is important for children to grow up in safety and to be able to participate in activities with other children without having to interfere with the economy. Unfortunately, the National Audit Office’s study shows that developments seem to have gone in the wrong direction. Instead of having fewer poor children, there have been more. So here we still have a big and important job to do together.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e1711	Although the Control and Constitutional Committee has provided an almost unanimous position on the matter under consideration, it has a serious backlash. Nearly 80,000 children live in poverty in the world’s richest country. There are numbers we can't be familiar with, but they've been stable in recent years. Unfortunately, we do not see any decline in spite of the seemingly unanimous agreement – and agreed – that we must do something about this. The National Audit Office's report shows some very serious findings: Several children grow up in low-income families. Many municipalities do not do enough to allow poor children to participate socially. State efforts to reduce the impact of poverty among children and young people are too poorly coordinated. The needs of children are not well enough identified when parents apply for social assistance. This is not good. The question is whether the measures proposed by the Riksrevision are sufficient to address the same findings. I'm afraid the answer to that question is no. More knowledge and better coordination are, of course, important, but hardly enough. I would therefore like to spend some of my time in my contribution also pointing to measures that I and the Left believe will be important for fewer children to grow up in poverty in Norway in the future. I want to look forward. We need to use energy to find good measures that work. Poverty in Norway is not just about having a lower standard of living, but about standing outside society and excluding the possibility of participating in the various communities. While pensioners were one of the largest low-income groups in the past, today young people, young adults, lone-parents and, in particular, families with immigrant backgrounds account for a higher proportion. In 2012, half of the children in low-income families in Norway had a migrant background. There are several reasons why the number of poor children has increased. The evolution of income has led to a higher low income threshold. Increased immigration has helped to increase the number of children in low-income families. There has also been an increase in the number of lone dependants with low incomes. However, the main cause of low income is lack of professional participation. The labour market is and will be the main venue for the distribution of income and protection against poverty. Although it is an overall objective to be able to care for themselves as much as possible through work, there will always be people who, for various reasons, cannot be in regular employment. Then we have to make sure that we have good arrangements for people who can't take care of themselves. At the same time, such income insurance must not be designed in such a way that it does not pay to work for those who can work. It's a demanding balance. The left wants to lift more of today's poor out of poverty and at the same time prevent future poverty. We therefore need to take action in several areas. We will give priority to action in the fields of education, employment, housing, health and inclusion. Some of these, as a result of the budgetary agreement between the government parties, the Left and the Christian People's Party, will fortunately become the current policy from next year. In order to reduce poverty, school policies must ensure that everyone acquires basic literacy skills, that social challenges are followed up early, and that more people are qualified for work. Failure to complete secondary education presents a significant risk of future exclusion. Persons who have not completed secondary education are overrepresented among those who are dependent on public benefits. Many suggest that the dropout starts early and often due to lack of basic skills already from primary school. In addition, follow-up at home is important. For parents, basic language skills are important, not just for getting a job, but for following up the children who are being trained. That is how we can prevent poverty from inheriting. Kindergarten is an important arena for the development of language and social skills which should also give children of parents with low incomes the opportunity to participate. In particular, children are living on reception facilities, which are currently not entitled to kindergarten. It will be a challenge for future Norway if many of the children living in receptions do not learn Norwegian, and then start in Norwegian school, with the danger of falling out of school that unfortunately very often has consequences. In particular, the left will give priority to free core time in kindergarten for 3 to 5 year-olds who are children of low-income parents, differentiated parental pay in the school leave scheme/activity school, more nurses and specialised educators and better school counselling. The left will also implement a language training reform through adult training. Everyone who has children in kindergarten, or has had children in kindergarten or in the school leisure system sees that there are significant benefits from having children in kindergarten and attending preschool before starting school. Both the ability to interact with other children, the social added value of going to kindergarten, and also the ability to communicate with other children in the Norwegian common language, provide great benefits. We know that health centres are also understaffed very many places in Norway. Here we need a stepping-up plan to get more health sisters in the school health service, but also in health centres. It is also linked to the fact that we know that poverty and, in particular, child poverty – and studies show this – are related to violence and violence against children. And we know that detecting the risk factors, detecting care failures early, is very important for making an offer and taking early action. The hearings we have had at Stortinget show that special children between zero and one year of age are severely affected by care failure, as well as children from one to five years of age. This is before they start in ordinary school. If several of these children are in kindergarten, and if we have an enhanced health station service, we will be able to do something about the poverty gap in the long term, and also help those children who are most resource-responsible and most at risk to be exposed to care failure. The most important means of preventing poverty is to ensure that people get into work or keep work. Work gives individual economic autonomy and is at the same time the most important area of social inclusion. Persons aged 25 to 65 who live in a household without a permanent professional relationship are five times more likely to have persistent low incomes than all others in the same age group. In recent years, Nav has been criticised by several teams for the fact that the life situation of the children is not monitored when parents apply for social assistance. It is also repeated in the Riksrevision study that we are now discussing. This applies, for example: the children’s need for clothing, shoes and equipment, as well as whether the family can afford to participate in various activities. Therefore, there appears to be a need to clarify to Nav the importance of monitoring the needs of children. At the same time, there is a need for more room for local discretion and freedom of action in Nav to follow up and develop measures for those who are out of work for various reasons. In particular, the left will give priority to reducing the threshold in working life by softening access to temporary employment, implementing measures to help disadvantaged people enter the labour market, increasing subsidies for social entrepreneurship and strengthening the arrangements for organising grants and temporary wage subsidies for job seekers with disabilities. The left will also introduce experiments on anonymous applications in the public sector and enhance the quality of the qualification programme by making it less arbitrary and more targeted. Researchers at Fafo Jon Rogstad in 2012 sent out anonymous, or fictitious, applications, a total of 1,800, and the only thing that separated them was the names of the applicants. So it was a typical ethnic Norwegian name and a typical, what to say, immigrant name, not a typical ethnic Norwegian name. We sometimes call them brown Northmen, such as me – that is, those types of names, which are slightly different, which are sometimes difficult to pronounce. Everything else was similar. Significant differences in how to be called for an interview depend on your name. Everything else is equal to. It's alarming. You may have been born and raised in Norway, but by your name you will not even be called for an interview, even if you have a university education or what you now have when applying for a job. We in the Left believe that an attempt at anonymous applications in the public sector could contribute to remedying some of these discriminations to which people with a migrant background are exposed, i.e. the group with the largest number of children living in poverty in Norway.
