However, the National Audit Office points out that there is a need for more knowledge and competence on the consequences of poverty among children and young people in municipalities and more knowledge of measures and methods that can contribute to better opportunities for social participation for more people. This year I have commissioned the Directorate to assist municipalities in developing indicators on child poverty. This is an important work. Municipalities need to know which poor children and young people and families need action and then target their efforts. The National Audit Office writes that only one in 10 municipalities have evaluated their actions, and there is a lack of knowledge of methods and good solutions in the work. The Directorate will now consider how to establish good arenas for the exchange of experience between municipalities and non-governmental organisations in the field of child poverty. The Executive Board will also assess how the need for evaluation of actions and plans under the National Disability Grant Scheme can be implemented. Contributions to action against poverty among children, young people and families with children, managed by the Directorate of Labour, were evaluated in 2009. The evaluation showed that the interventions have contributed to the inclusion of children and young people in different activities and in social arenas. Subsidies have also led to increased awareness in municipalities of poverty and social exclusion among children and young people. The same findings were made in the 2010 evaluation of the response to poverty within the grant scheme Children’s and young people’s actions in larger urban communities, managed by the BLD. The national audit suggests that better coordination of State efforts to reduce the impact of poverty among children and young people is ensured. They recommend better cooperation between the Directorates with tasks for children and young people at risk of poverty. I am pleased that both my directorate and the Directorate of Labour have already started cooperation on grant management and work against child poverty. The Minister of Labour and Social Affairs and I will entrust the Directorates with the task of further developing this cooperation. We will also inform the Directorates to consider how to coordinate and simplify State grant schemes against child poverty. A large proportion of children who grow up in families with poor advice have a migrant background, which has been discussed by many. The Directorate will now assess how the IMDi can be involved and contribute to the cooperation between the other two Directorates, as also pointed out by the National Audit Office. It'll be an important job next year, so you can cooperate much better. The National Audit Office points out that there is a need to clarify in which cases financial support for recreational activities is to be provided under the Law on the Protection of Children or the Law on Social Services of Nav. We have started work on common guidelines for cooperation and sharing of responsibilities between the Child Protection Service and the Labour Directorate. Bufdir and the Directorate of Labour will be given a joint mandate to follow this up in 2015. The National Audit Office points out that the needs of the children are not adequately addressed when parents apply for social assistance. It is very important that children’s needs be looked after and cared for when families with children receive financial support from Nav. A countrywide surveillance in 2012 revealed that a number of Nav offices did not take sufficient account of children in the proceedings. Following supervision, the county councillors are now systematically following up the deviations in the municipalities. The requirement for individual assessments and management's obligation to demand and monitor the needs of children is addressed in a circular to the Law on Social Services in Nav. However, the government believes that there is an important work, and the report available from the National Audit Office will provide good working tools for what are the challenges and where we are going to put the work. We also believe that we need to strengthen the position of families in Norwegian society. Early work is therefore important, and everyone in the municipalities works across – from health centres to kindergartens, to schools, to child protection and to family protection. We have to work together to get well-functioning families, which are for the benefit of the children. Research shows that low-income families and children are at risk. The government has also announced a family announcement in the spring of 2016, where we will focus on the family's importance for children's growing-up conditions. In this communication, we will also look at measures to support the family and establish a safety net around when the family fails.

It must be legitimate to use extended collection time to reduce the annual costs of the drivers. The third is the use of a calculation rate, which is above both the floating rate and the fixed rate for loans with a county guarantee. Such practices, of course, involve a reduction in the risk of borrowing. The result is that most of the projects are disbursed in advance. But here, too, we are facing the issue of whether it is desirable that today's drivers pay overprice for a long-term project.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e2478	The starting point for the case is the proposal from the representatives Heikki Eidsvoll Holmås and Abid Q. Raja to ensure the county councils have the opportunity to impose environmental requirements on the taxi industry. The Committee has noted that the proposers are seeking amendments to the professional transport legislation in order to provide the basis for the county councils to impose specific environmental requirements on new taxis. Today, the Professional Transport Act does not authorise either the Permit Authority or the Ministry to impose such requirements on the taxi industry. Based on our climate policy objectives set out in the Stortinget, the Committee agrees that all measures that can have a positive impact on air quality improvements, in cities in particular, should be assessed positively. Municipalities and county councils should be given a space for action in order to achieve their environmental policy objectives. Here, too, the state has an important role to play in helping to create just such a space of action. As the Council of State writes in its letter to the Committee, municipalities, including county councils, have been given a number of tasks and instruments to influence sources of environmentally harmful emissions. Municipalities are policy actors, service providers, regulators, purchasers, property owners and responsible for planning and facilitating a good living environment for their inhabitants. It is therefore not unnatural that opportunities should also be provided for setting environmental requirements for an industry that accounts for a non-negligible proportion of emissions. There are also, as we see today, some positive features in the picture. The emissions of new vehicles, Euro6, appear to have a clear beneficial effect on the total emissions. This applies not least to the heavier vehicles. Calculations from the Transport Economic Institute show that the emissions of NO2 in the Oslo area — provided that it is a natural vehicle replacement — will actually decrease from 2015 onwards. In the opinion of the Committee, provision should be made for the possibility of imposing requirements on zero and low-emission cars for new taxis from county municipalities. However, there is a need for a transitional period before any new requirements are introduced. This is necessary in order to take the necessary account of the taxi industry, and not least for the industry to be given predictability. If more stringent requirements are introduced for new taxis quickly, this may result in such a high cost increase for the taxi industry itself that, instead of rapidly replacing older taxis, one chooses to postpone the replacement of the car fleet. This would have resulted in an increase in emissions rather than a reduction, which would be very negative both for the environment and for industry. The Council of State otherwise informs the Committee by letter that it is a cross-ministerial working group which is now in the final phase of its investigation. The group shall: ‘Evaluate the need for instruments to reach EU limit values and assess the possible instruments. would be appropriate to introduce, including the effectiveness of measures and costs for public and private persons.’;» The Committee meets a case for the Storting, which sets out the ways in which county municipalities can be given the opportunity to demand zero- and low-emission cars. At the same time, it is important to explain how to facilitate predictable and appropriate transitional arrangements for the taxi industry. The majority of the Committee is concerned that the county should be given the possibility to impose conditions on zero and low-emission cars for new taxis. The majority, the Labour Party, the Right, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the Left therefore support the proposal proposed by the representative, which reads as follows: ‘The government asks the government to ensure that county councils have the opportunity to impose zero and low-emission conditions on new taxis through Section 11 of the Traffic Act.’ I therefore recommend the Committee's recommendation.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e3005	The issue is to incorporate the Patient Rights Directive in order to facilitate the provision of high-quality healthcare across national borders and to contribute to cooperation between EEA Member States in the field of health. Norway has already established a reimbursement scheme for health care expenditure received in other EEA countries, but now it is proposed to extend it to hospital treatment, i.e. the specialist healthcare service. The majority dealt with the Patient Directive in the spring of 2013, but then with the requirement for prior approval. The current government did not decide to implement the decision and sent it a new hearing on 27 June 2014 proposing not to require prior approval, but with the possibility of applying for prior approval. The Committee has shared its opinion. With subsidiary voting, there is a majority for continued requirement for prior approval and the previous Grandfathering Decision of 2013 is promoted again. The minority supports the government's proposal. After the matter had been settled within the Committee, one of the parties behind the majority has notified that they consider to change their position when dealing with the matter in the Stortinget. I hereby explain the views of the Workers' Party. The working party responds to the government's decision to circumvent the Storting's previous 2013 decision and the Health Ministry's previous recommendation on prior approval. We believe that the government would consult Stortinget before submitting proposals to the EEA to remove the prior approval. We also react to the patient directive being used to introduce a general access for hospitals in the EEA area to receive funding for Norwegian patients. We believe that free treatment choice is a major issue, which deserves a broad investigation and thorough discussion in Stortinget, as the government has also announced. The working party believes that opening up to financing hospital stays in the EEA without prior approval is the wrong use of Community health resources. We think it's more important to strengthen hospitals here at home. It seems unwise to give commercial hospitals in the EU a greater right to receive treatment for Norwegian patients than for similar hospitals in Norway. The Norwegian healthcare system is based on a publicly funded health service, unlike most other EEA countries, where many have insurance-based systems. This disparity in organisation and funding, together with the fact that Norway has a very good economy compared to other European countries, allows Norwegian patients to be economically attractive to many European hospitals. In order to avoid our healthcare being deprived of resources, which in the next round may have consequences for the quality of the health service nationally, the Labour Party believes it is wise to lead was. We believe that prior approval is best for the patient. Lack of prior authorisation may quickly become an additional burden on patients and their relatives because they are at risk of being subject to disagreement on the basis of reimbursement in retrospect. There are many factors that could lead to disagreement about costs. There is a lack of comparable price lists for many treatment services, and very many hospitals will press for the best possible payment for Norwegian patients. Disagreements may arise as to whether patients are subject to time-limits in Norway in order to meet the conditions for receiving an equivalent health offer in the EEA area and as to whether the health offer abroad is considered equivalent to the health offer of the patient here in the home country. The working party is also concerned that a right to treatment without prior authorisation will favour the resource-intensive patients at the expense of those who are so ill that they cannot travel abroad or cannot afford to advance hospital stays abroad. A prior approval of reimbursement of hospital treatment costs will be an important tool to gain control of patient flow out of the country and the economy. Then we also have that with antibiotic resistance, which is an increasing problem, and the risk increases with hospitalization abroad. Pre-approval may facilitate the monitoring of antibiotic resistance by Norway. The Labour Party is in favour of European cooperation, especially because it enables us to offer the best treatment for rare conditions. This mechanism, which was negotiated and on the basis of previous decisions, was good. Now we feel that it is diluted by the sitting government. Finally, I would like to address the proposal that the Labour Party is behind.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e3038	The Patient Rights Directive clarifies patients’ right to reimbursement of the costs of healthcare that the patient has chosen to provide in another EEA country. This is a regulation of rights that the European Court of Justice has already established that patients have. Norway is now implementing the Patient Directive. The reimbursement scheme under the Directive will primarily concern the planned treatment, so that it will be particularly relevant in cases where patients have conducted studies in advance on where they can obtain a good treatment offer. Access shall also cover expenditure limited to healthcare that patients will also be covered in Norway. To clarify, this applies to patients who already have a right to health care. So it's not like patients will have treatment that's experimental or experimental. The new Directive provides an important opportunity for some patients. Some patients have only one offer in Norway; they do not have the option of choosing another offer today. Others want to choose a treatment provision in another country for other reasons. This Directive therefore strengthens the rights of patients. It follows from the Patient Rights Directive that service providers should provide patients with relevant information on treatment options, on the very provision, quality and safety of healthcare services. In addition, the country of treatment has an obligation to ensure the existence of complaints and redress procedures. The Right Party, the Advance Party and the Left support the possibility for Norwegian patients to recover the costs of hospital treatment in another EEA country. Whether or not the patient has applied in advance shall not prevent the patient from obtaining his/her rights as long as the treatment meets the requirements we otherwise impose in Norway. In the consultation note with proposals for regulatory amendments sent out in June of this year, it was not proposed to impose a requirement for prior approval. However, it is clear that the Department will follow this development over time. And if, in some areas, major patient flows create problems in maintaining a qualitatively good health supply in Norway or it contributes to poor use of resources, the Ministry will assess whether to impose prior authorisation requirements in the relevant areas. I would like to stress that all patient organisations that have expressed their views on the government's proposal support this. This means that Nordics have the same opportunities as patients in Sweden to cover their costs if they choose hospital treatment in another EEA country, and I stress that there is a relatively small group of patients. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e3101	For the Centre Party, equivalent health services to the entire population, regardless of socio-economic strength, are one of the pillars of the welfare community. The Patient Rights Directive is part of a market right for the healthcare sector that challenges this principle and the obligation of Storting and the right to control health priorities in the future. The Directive states that patients should be entitled to receive health care in all countries that are covered by the EU internal market. The patient should be entitled to reimbursement up to the cost of treatment in the home country of the patient. Treatment costs are reimbursed to the patient if this treatment is also offered in the home country to the patient. In principle, free treatment options are introduced in Europe. In the opinion on the free choice of treatment, the Health Directorate refers precisely to the close relationship between the introduction of free treatment options and the Patient Rights Directive. The Health Directorate points out that the consequences of free treatment options for the public health service cannot be assessed in isolation. The size must look at the overall impact of the introduction of free treatment options and the directive on patients’ rights and the market alignment of healthcare that these reforms will lead to together. Being against free treatment options and for the Patient Rights Directive, we believe that there is a blind loyalty to the EEA Agreement. The Patient Rights Directive is not about giving rare diagnostics access to an international professional environment, as some try to produce it. International medical-professional cooperation is not a reinforcement of this Directive. The decisions of the Directive on cooperation between Member States in the field of health are based on the voluntary approach. While increased cooperation is voluntary, if the Directive is implemented, it is a duty to increase the market and free patient mobility between national borders. The main objective of the Directive is to ensure the free movement of services in a single European health market, where public and private providers are treated as equivalent nationally. This health market will then be harmonised with an EU/EEA cross-border market. The fact that rare diseases can be prioritised in a market system is unlikely. These patients will not be attractive or profitable in the health market created by the Directive. The Patient Rights Directive has been presented in the debate as a right for patients to have access to rare treatments that they will not be able to obtain in their home country. There is a false presentation of the facts. The Directive states in paragraphs 13, 31 and 33 that the obligation to cover costs of cross-border healthcare is limited to the healthcare that the patient is required to provide and that exists in the home country. Both the government and the EU ensure that today’s negligible level of cross-border healthcare will continue. Nevertheless, the purpose of the Directive is, under point 2, to ‘improve the functioning of the internal market and the free movement of goods and services’. The purpose of the Directive is therefore to facilitate the growth of this health market. The bill we are dealing with came after pressure from ESA, and had said that the monitoring body was not satisfied with the pre-approval system for the previous Stortinget. When things were up in Stortinget in the previous period, I said the following in my post: ‘In principle, national legislation is subject to EU law in all areas covered by the decisions on the free movement of services. While a territory is being opened, legal developments in this area will be determined by the Court of Justice of the European Union. Today’s Norwegian interpretation of the Directive will therefore be able to be corrected by decisions of the European Court of Justice.’ Someone says that the centre party has turned around when we now go against the directive. I would rather say that we take the consequence that the EU system has responded as we feared and pointed out, and believe that in today's situation the discussion is about pre-approval or not one of the skin debates between the Labour Party and the Right in particular. I would like to mention the proposal of the Central Party and the Socialist Left Party in the configuration. The centre party will reject the inclusion of the Patient Rights Directive.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e3131	The Patient Rights Directive clarifies and regulates patients’ right to reimbursement of healthcare costs received in another EEA country. The Directive makes practical arrangements for patients to use healthcare in other Member States, and the Left is pleased that the current reimbursement scheme for healthcare provision in the EEA area is now being extended to hospital treatment. This will make it easier to ensure high-quality cross-border healthcare and contribute to cooperation between Member States in the field of health. The left has – to some degree of doubt – stated that it is correct not to require prior authorisation for reimbursement of hospital expenses. Experience has shown that there is a limited number of patients who choose to use the freedom of choice to travel to another EEA country for treatment. From our own free hospital choice, there are also experienced few who opt outside their own company. It is therefore considered inappropriate to establish a bureaucratic system for the presumed low number of patients who will leave, which only helps to extend the waiting time for patients seeking treatment. However, we in the Left will follow developments over time and come back to the question if it turns out to be a more costly system than what we see today. In this respect, it is important to bear in mind that a prerequisite for covering the costs of treatment in another EEA country is that health care equivalent to the health care patient would be paid by the public in Norway. The reimbursement is limited to what health care would have cost the public in this country. For the Left, it is important that patients who have been assessed as entitled to the necessary health care from the specialist healthcare services under the Patient and User Rights Act should be able to apply for prior commitments. This ensures that the costs will be reimbursed afterwards, which will be particularly important for cost-intensive treatments. However, this access is also important to prevent the possibility of choosing treatment abroad not only from being used by resource-intensive patients. The right to cover costs of treatment abroad must not be reserved for those who can afford to advance hospital stays abroad. However, in the case of treatments that are clearly covered by the reimbursement scheme, prior authorisation would prevent the effective implementation of the treatment. The left is concerned that we should continue to have international cooperation on patient treatment, which helps to strengthen the professional competence of Norwegian hospitals and provides patients with better treatment. It is important that we also have wide professional competence in Norway, and that we have the ambition to be able to offer all patients. However, there will be patients with rare conditions that are now more likely to be offered treatment at hospitals in countries where acute competences have been developed in the area in question. These are patients who may not have time to wait for the necessary professional skills to be built up in Norway. Another example is those who want a ‘second opinion’ and who can easily access it through a wider environment of competence in another country. The development of professional competence in Norway should not hinder the right of these patients to receive adequate treatment in a hospital competent in this area. The left will have good professionally funded public health services, and the quality of services is more important than who performs them. We will work to ensure that public, non-profit and private health care settings complement each other, and we believe that the possibility of receiving treatment abroad will help to ensure that more patients receive adequate treatment and that the quality of health care we offer to our patients in sum will be strengthened. The left therefore supports the government's legislative proposals.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-16.ana.ud1018e3162	The Patient Rights Directive was adopted in the EU in March 2011. Without prejudice to the consent of Storting, the Directive was incorporated into the EEA Agreement in the summer of 2014. This is the background for the government in the harvest promoting a consent proposition, Prop. 135 S for 2013–2014, to be dealt with today in Stortinget. The Patient Rights Directive clarifies patients’ right to reimbursement of healthcare costs that the patient has chosen to have carried out in another EU country. This is a regulation of rights already established by the Court of Justice of the European Union that patients have, as a result of the rules on the free movement of services laid down in the EU Treaty and the EEA Agreement. The Directive provides for the possibility of requiring prior hospital authorisation. However, an authorisation system must be limited to what is deemed necessary and proportionate and must not lead to arbitrary discrimination. The Directive also contains rules that make it practical for the patient to benefit from healthcare in other Member States. In addition, it provides for voluntary cooperation between countries. The formal requirements set out in the Directive are already largely covered by Norwegian health legislation. We have established a reimbursement scheme for healthcare received in other EEA countries that already covers the reimbursement rules of the Directive. However, the current reimbursement scheme does not include reimbursement of hospital expenses. I Prop. Therefore, 118 L for 2012–2013, Changes to the Patient and User Rights Act, etc., were proposed to extend the hospital treatment scheme. This was a proposal from the previous government. The size joined this. In addition, a provision of the People's Security Act to impose prior authorisation was adopted. In addition, some provisions were adopted which relate to patients from other EEA countries who wish to receive healthcare in Norway. This government puts the patient in the center. We have therefore re-evaluated the need for prior approval. In this context, we have weighed the need for healthcare planning and monitoring against patients’ needs and wishes for freedom of choice, short waiting and simple procedure. From the patient’s point of view, prior authorisation would limit the possibility of hospital treatment in other countries. Subject to prior authorisation, the patient will have the right to leave when he or she has to wait irresponsibly for a long time for the necessary hospital treatment in Norway. Such an obstacle will particularly affect patients with conditions where there is a small and limited professional environment in Norway. These patients will have reduced domestic choice and will have a particular need and desire to have access to medical competence in other European countries. That is the consequence of the minority's proposal in this case. Pre-authorisation is an instrument to limit patient flows to other EEA countries. The experience from Sweden, which has allowed patients to travel freely to other EEA countries since 2005, is that few patients choose to receive healthcare in other EEA countries. The Norwegian healthcare system is very good, and we're working to make it even better. In my opinion, there is no basis to believe that there will be very many patients who want to receive hospital treatment in other EEA countries. But those who want to choose it often have a very good reason for it. Our experience of free hospital choice in Norway is based on this assumption. It is, however, that we do not want patients to have to face a risk that they will not be able to meet the costs. That is why we are also setting up a pre-commitment system which allows patients who wish to receive a pre-commitment to cover the costs in line with the directive. The regulation and the system have been consulted. The five patient organisations that have expressed their opinion on these changes are all positive, including the Disability Joint Organisation, the Cancer Association and the Revmatik Association. Everyone is positive that this government is changing the system by removing prior approval and establishing a simple and orderly system for prior commitments. For me, that's the best proof that this system is a system that builds up under our vision of creating the patient's healthcare. Some of the submissions have tried to present it as if it were the threat from EFTA that we are proposing this. It's not. We have proposed this because we believe it is a good policy for the patient. It underpins the objective of the patient’s healthcare.

It shows that more students feel lonely, and that many students struggle with mastering. According to § 3-1 of the Health and Care Services Act, each municipality must ensure that persons staying in the municipality are provided with the necessary health and care services and, in order to fulfil this responsibility, the municipality must, inter alia,: provide a fixed medical system, medical watch and emergency medical service. This means that the municipality must also ensure that students, or others with permanent qualifications in a municipality other than the municipality of residence, have access to the necessary health care. Municipalities with many commuters and students etc. shall ensure, in the planning of medical services, that the decision-makers have overall capacity to receive them as well.
